What brings you in?\n\nPatient: Well, thank you. Um, I'm here because I've just been living with this constant mix of guilt, fear, and anger. It's been like this ever since my friend was killed. I just can't seem to turn that off.\n\nDoctor: Since your friend was killed, have you felt that what happened was your fault?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Um... I keep telling myself that if I had just picked a different route for us that day, or... or if I had pushed harder about avoiding that crossing, she might still be alive. It's hard not to blame myself. And then... then I just get furious all over again at the driver, and at the town for letting that intersection be so dangerous in the first place.\n\nDoctor: Since your friend was killed, have you lost interest in activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes, I have. Um... I used to do quite a bit, actually. I would plan out my garden beds, quilt with the ladies, go to theater outings... I even loved to argue happily at my book club. But now, I just look at those things on the calendar and... I feel almost nothing. It's just like they belong to someone I used to be.\n\nDoctor: Since your friend was killed, have you been feeling down or depressed most days?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. Um... I wouldn't say I feel depressed or down most days. I know I just said I don't care much about my usual activities anymore, but it doesn't really feel like a deep sadness or anything like that. So, no, I haven't really noticed feeling depressed.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

It's like I can see my friend stepping off the curb all over again, and I just have to watch it happen before I can stop it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding places, sounds, or other reminders that bring back what happened to your friend?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Um, I actually go out of my way to avoid that specific intersection now. I just can't go near it. And I've stopped signing up for most tours... or even local safety meetings. I just can't bear it when the conversations inevitably circle back to what happened there. It's just easier to stay away.\n\nDoctor: Since your friend was killed, have you been feeling constantly on guard or easily startled?\n\nPatient: Yes, very much so. Um... especially if I have to be anywhere near traffic. I just find myself watching every single truck, every curb, and every pedestrian around me. It feels like... like it's my responsibility to catch any danger before it can happen again. I keep telling myself that if I had just picked a different route for us that day, or... or if I had pushed harder about avoiding that crossing, she might still be alive. It's just... it's always there, and it really hasn't eased up the way everyone seemed to think it would by now.\n\nDoctor: Since your friend was killed, have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: No, not really. It doesn't even take much to trigger it. Um, just hearing a crosswalk signal, or the growl of a heavy truck... even seeing a walking-tour map can suddenly put the whole scene right back in my head. It comes back so sharply. It's like I can see my friend stepping off the curb all over again, and I just have to watch it happen before I can stop it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding places, sounds, or other reminders that bring back what happened to your friend?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Um, I actually go out of my way to avoid that specific intersection now. I just can't go near it. And I've stopped signing up for most tours... or even local safety meetings. I just can't bear it when the conversations inevitably circle back to what happened there. It's just easier to stay away.\n\nDoctor: Since your friend was killed, have you been feeling constantly on guard or easily startled?\n\nPatient: Yes, very much so. Um... especially if I have to be anywhere near traffic. I just find myself watching every single truck, every curb, and every pedestrian around me. It feels like... like it's my responsibility to catch any danger before it can happen again. I just can't seem to turn that off.\n\nDoctor: Since your friend was killed, have you felt that what happened was your fault?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Um... I keep telling myself that if I had just picked a different route for us that day, or... or if I had pushed harder about avoiding that crossing, she might still be alive. It's hard not to blame myself. And then... then I just get furious all over again at the driver, and at the town for letting that intersection be so dangerous in the first place.\n\nDoctor: Since your friend was killed, have you lost interest in activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes, I have. Um... I used to do quite a bit, actually. I would plan out my garden beds, quilt with the ladies, go to theater outings... I even loved to argue happily at my book club. But now, I just look at those things on the calendar and... I feel almost nothing. It's just like they belong to someone I used to be.\n\nDoctor: Since your friend was killed, have you been feeling down or depressed most days?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. Um... I wouldn't say I feel depressed or down most days. I know I just said I don't care much about my usual activities anymore, but it doesn't really feel like a deep sadness or anything like that. So, no, I haven't really noticed feeling depressed.\n\nDoctor: Have these symptoms been going on for more than a month?\n\nPatient: Yes, they have. Um... this has all been going on since she was killed on that walking tour. That was about two years and three months ago now.\n\nDoctor: Since your friend's death, have you found yourself believing that the world is unsafe?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. I mean, obviously, I know terrible things can happen—what happened to her certainly proved that. But I don't walk around thinking the whole world is completely dangerous or out to get me. I just... I haven't really had that specific feeling.\n\nDoctor: Since your friend was killed, have you been feeling strong emotions like fear, anger, guilt, or shame much of the time?\n\nPatient: Yes, I have. Actually, that's a big part of what brings me in to see you. I've just been living with this constant mix of guilt, fear, and anger ever since she was killed. It's just... it's always there, and it really hasn't eased up the way everyone seemed to think it would by now.\n\nDoctor: Since your friend was killed, have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I've actually been sleeping alright. I mean, I might have an off night here and there like anyone else, but nothing out of the ordinary. Sleep hasn't really been a problem for me.\n\nDoctor: Did you actually witness your friend being killed?\n\nPatient: No, I didn't see it happen. I wasn't there when... when it actually occurred. I just got the call and found out about it afterwards.\n\nDoctor: Since your friend died, have you had nightmares about what happened?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't. Like I mentioned, my sleep has been pretty normal. So, no, I haven't been having any bad dreams or nightmares about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself yearning or longing for your friend?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I miss him, naturally, because we were good friends. But I wouldn't say I'm yearning or longing for him. I know he's gone, and... well, I just don't have that kind of intense feeling about it. It's just a very sad thing that happened.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.23, F41.9, F43.21\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, I’m glad you came in today. What brings you in?\n\nPatient: Well, thank you. Um, I'm here because I've just been living with this constant mix of guilt, fear, and anger. It's been like this ever since my friend was killed. People kept telling me it would get easier with time, but... it just hasn't eased up the way everyone seemed to think it would.\n\nDoctor: Do memories of your friend's death ever suddenly come back in a vivid, upsetting way, like you’re reliving it?\n\nPatient: Yes, they do. It doesn't even take much to trigger it. Um, just hearing a crosswalk signal, or the growl of a heavy truck... even seeing a walking-tour map can suddenly put the whole scene right back in my head. It comes back so sharply. It's like I can see my friend stepping off the curb all over again, and I just have to watch it happen before I can stop it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding places, sounds, or other reminders that bring back what happened to your friend?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Um, I actually go out of my way to avoid that specific intersection now. I just can't go near it. And I've stopped signing up for most tours... or even local safety meetings. I just can't bear it when the conversations inevitably circle back to what happened there. It's just easier to stay away.\n\nDoctor: Since your friend was killed, have you been feeling constantly on guard or easily startled?\n\nPatient: Yes, very much so. Um... especially if I have to be anywhere near traffic. I just find myself watching every single truck, every curb, and every pedestrian around me. It feels like... like it's my responsibility to catch any danger before it can happen again. I just can't seem to turn that off.\n\nDoctor: Since your friend was killed, have you felt that what happened was your fault?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Um... I keep telling myself that if I had just picked a different route for us that day, or... or if I had pushed harder about avoiding that crossing, she might still be alive. It's hard not to blame myself. And then... then I just get furious all over again at the driver, and at the town for letting that intersection be so dangerous in the first place.\n\nDoctor: Since your friend was killed, have you lost interest in activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes, I have. Um... I used to do quite a bit, actually. I would plan out my garden beds, quilt with the ladies, go to theater outings... I even loved to argue happily at my book club. But now, I just look at those things on the calendar and... I feel almost nothing. It's just like they belong to someone I used to be.\n\nDoctor: Since your friend was killed, have you been feeling down or depressed most days?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. Um... I wouldn't say I feel depressed or down most days. I know I just said I don't care much about my usual activities anymore, but it doesn't really feel like a deep sadness or anything like that. So, no, I haven't really noticed feeling depressed.\n\nDoctor: Have these symptoms been going on for more than a month?\n\nPatient: Yes, they have. Um... this has all been going on since she was killed on that walking tour. That was about two years and three months ago now.\n\nDoctor: Since your friend's death, have you found yourself believing that the world is unsafe?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. I mean, obviously, I know terrible things can happen—what happened to her certainly proved that. But I don't walk around thinking the whole world is completely dangerous or out to get me. I just... I haven't really had that specific feeling.\n\nDoctor: Since your friend was killed, have you been feeling strong emotions like fear, anger, guilt, or shame much of the time?\n\nPatient: Yes, I have. Actually, that's a big part of what brings me in to see you. I've just been living with this constant mix of guilt, fear, and anger ever since she was killed. It's just... it's always there, and it really hasn't eased up the way everyone seemed to think it would by now.\n\nDoctor: Since your friend was killed, have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I've actually been sleeping alright. I mean, I might have an off night here and there like anyone else, but nothing out of the ordinary. Sleep hasn't really been a problem for me.\n\nDoctor: Did you actually witness your friend being killed?\n\nPatient: No, I didn't see it happen. I wasn't there when... when it actually occurred. I just got the call and found out about it afterwards.\n\nDoctor: Since your friend died, have you had nightmares about what happened?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't. Like I mentioned, my sleep has been pretty normal. So, no, I haven't been having any bad dreams or nightmares about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself yearning or longing for your friend?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I miss him, naturally, because we were good friends. But I wouldn't say I'm yearning or longing for him. I know he's gone, and... well, I just don't have that kind of intense feeling about it. It's just a very sad thing that happened.\n\nDoctor: When those memories come back, does it ever feel as if you are actually there watching it happen in real time, rather than just thinking about it?\n\nPatient: No, it doesn't feel like that. Since I wasn't actually there when it happened, I don't really have a memory of the event itself to... well, to watch play out. When the memories come back, it's just me remembering getting that phone call, or just thinking about the fact that he's gone. But no, I never feel like I'm actually there or reliving it in real time.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or worried even when nothing reminds you of your friend's death?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I don't really get anxious or worried out of the blue like that. I mean, I have the usual everyday concerns, but I'm not just sitting around feeling anxious for no reason when I'm not thinking about him.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. It doesn't even take much to trigger it. Um, just hearing a crosswalk signal, or the growl of a heavy truck... even seeing a walking-tour map can suddenly put the whole scene right back in my head. It comes back so sharply. It's like I can see my friend stepping off the curb all over again, and I just have to watch it happen before I can stop it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding places, sounds, or other reminders that bring back what happened to your friend?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Um, I actually go out of my way to avoid that specific intersection now. I just can't go near it. And I've stopped signing up for most tours... or even local safety meetings. I just can't bear it when the conversations inevitably circle back to what happened there. It's just easier to stay away.\n\nDoctor: Since your friend was killed, have you been feeling constantly on guard or easily startled?\n\nPatient: Yes, very much so. I keep telling myself that if I had just picked a different route for us that day, or... or if I had pushed harder about avoiding that crossing, she might still be alive. But no, I never feel like I'm actually there or reliving it in real time.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or worried even when nothing reminds you of your friend's death?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I don't really get anxious or worried out of the blue like that. I mean, I have the usual everyday concerns, but I'm not just sitting around feeling anxious for no reason when I'm not thinking about him.\n\nDoctor: Did these feelings start within three months after your friend died?\n\nPatient: I'm not entirely sure, to be honest. It's... well, it's hard to put an exact timeline on it. I mean, I was grieving right away, of course, but I couldn't tell you if the things we've been talking about started exactly within that three-month window. It all just kind of blends together when you lose someone.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave these feelings been interfering a lot with your daily life?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 69 / not_in_workforce / bachelors\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Functional Impairment\nmanifestation: \"I have had to step back from organizing senior-center programs and managing volunteer budgets because I cannot keep up reliably, and I have pulled away from the clubs and activities that used to structure my week.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Plain patient.\nA plain patient describes their symptoms and experiences in a direct and straightforward manner, providing clear and relevant answers to the doctor's questions.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Um... I used to do quite a bit, actually. I would plan out my garden beds, quilt with the ladies, go to theater outings... I even loved to argue happily at my book club. I don't really get anxious or worried out of the blue like that. Um... especially if I have to be anywhere near traffic. I just find myself watching every single truck, every curb, and every pedestrian around me. It feels like... like it's my responsibility to catch any danger before it can happen again. I just can't seem to turn that off.\n\nDoctor: Since your friend was killed, have you felt that what happened was your fault?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Um... I keep telling myself that if I had just picked a different route for us that day, or... or if I had pushed harder about avoiding that crossing, she might still be alive. It's hard not to blame myself. And then... then I just get furious all over again at the driver, and at the town for letting that intersection be so dangerous in the first place.\n\nDoctor: Since your friend was killed, have you lost interest in activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes, I have. Um... I used to do quite a bit, actually. I would plan out my garden beds, quilt with the ladies, go to theater outings... I even loved to argue happily at my book club. But now, I just look at those things on the calendar and... I feel almost nothing. It's just like they belong to someone I used to be.\n\nDoctor: Since your friend was killed, have you been feeling down or depressed most days?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. Um... I wouldn't say I feel depressed or down most days. I know I just said I don't care much about my usual activities anymore, but it doesn't really feel like a deep sadness or anything like that. So, no, I haven't really noticed feeling depressed.\n\nDoctor: Have these symptoms been going on for more than a month?\n\nPatient: Yes, they have. Um... this has all been going on since she was killed on that walking tour. That was about two years and three months ago now.\n\nDoctor: Since your friend's death, have you found yourself believing that the world is unsafe?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. I mean, obviously, I know terrible things can happen—what happened to her certainly proved that. But I don't walk around thinking the whole world is completely dangerous or out to get me. I just... I haven't really had that specific feeling.\n\nDoctor: Since your friend was killed, have you been feeling strong emotions like fear, anger, guilt, or shame much of the time?\n\nPatient: Yes, I have. Actually, that's a big part of what brings me in to see you. I've just been living with this constant mix of guilt, fear, and anger ever since she was killed. It's just... it's always there, and it really hasn't eased up the way everyone seemed to think it would by now.\n\nDoctor: Since your friend was killed, have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I've actually been sleeping alright. I mean, I might have an off night here and there like anyone else, but nothing out of the ordinary. Sleep hasn't really been a problem for me.\n\nDoctor: Did you actually witness your friend being killed?\n\nPatient: No, I didn't see it happen. I wasn't there when... when it actually occurred. I just got the call and found out about it afterwards.\n\nDoctor: Since your friend died, have you had nightmares about what happened?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't. Like I mentioned, my sleep has been pretty normal. So, no, I haven't been having any bad dreams or nightmares about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself yearning or longing for your friend?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I miss him, naturally, because we were good friends. But I wouldn't say I'm yearning or longing for him. I know he's gone, and... well, I just don't have that kind of intense feeling about it. It's just a very sad thing that happened.\n\nDoctor: When those memories come back, does it ever feel as if you are actually there watching it happen in real time, rather than just thinking about it?\n\nPatient: No, it doesn't feel like that. Since I wasn't actually there when it happened, I don't really have a memory of the event itself to... well, to watch play out. When the memories come back, it's just me remembering getting that phone call, or just thinking about the fact that he's gone. But no, I never feel like I'm actually there or reliving it in real time.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or worried even when nothing reminds you of your friend's death?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I don't really get anxious or worried out of the blue like that. I mean, I have the usual everyday concerns, but I'm not just sitting around feeling anxious for no reason when I'm not thinking about him.\n\nDoctor: Did these feelings start within three months after your friend died?\n\nPatient: I'm not entirely sure, to be honest. It's... well, it's hard to put an exact timeline on it. I mean, I was grieving right away, of course, but I couldn't tell you if the things we've been talking about started exactly within that three-month window. It all just kind of blends together when you lose someone.\n\nDoctor: Have these feelings been interfering a lot with your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes, they have. I've actually had to step back from a lot of the things I used to do. I used to organize programs at the senior center and help manage the budgets for our volunteers, but I just... I can't keep up with it reliably anymore. I've also pulled away from the clubs and activities that used to give my week some structure. I just don't have it in me right now.\n\nDoctor: After your friend died, did these problems begin within the first three months?\n\nPatient: I think you just asked me that... but like I mentioned a moment ago, I'm really not sure. It's hard to pinpoint exactly when the grief turned into these other issues. I just couldn't tell you for certain if it was within those first three months. It's all just a blur.\n\nDoctor: Since your friend died, have you felt stuck in your grief, like you have trouble moving on or accepting the death?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. I mean, I miss him terribly, of course, but I know he's gone. I don't feel stuck in the grief itself, or like I can't accept that he passed away. It's more just... these other feelings and reactions we've been talking about that are making things so difficult. But I've accepted that he's gone.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
